If S="()" should be Yes even though only one color. The definition says colorful bracket sequence if possible to turn empty; no requirement to use all colors. So stack.

Let's consider if "colorful" might mean each adjacent pair deletion changes the color of neighboring brackets? No.

Now, let's maybe think about a more complex example: S = "([)]" no. S = "[([]])" no. S = "([<])>"? Let's test stack: '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'? Actually "([<])>" = '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'. Stack: '(' push, '[' push, '<' push, ']' top '<' mismatch -> fail. No adjacent pair? "<]" no, "])" no. Fail. Good.
S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.
S = "([<>) ]"? no.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm can be implemented with a list and dictionary. Good.

Let's consider if there is any need to handle `S` possibly containing `\r\n`; `strip()` removes `\r`. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "possibly zero" operations: If S is empty, stack empty -> Yes. But constraints length >=1. If S is a single opening, final stack not empty -> No. Good.

Let's think about if there is a need to use `ord` or something? No.

Now, let's maybe consider a potential counterexample to stack algorithm from the rewriting system's confluence: The stack algorithm fails on `())(` because after processing `()` stack empty, then `)` fails. But the word `())(` has a redex at positions 1-2. If we reduce it, we get `)(`, irreducible. If we reduce a different redex? There is no other. So not reducible. Good.
What about `())(()`? Stack fails at second `)`. Reducible? Delete first `()` -> `)(()`; then delete last `()` -> `)(`; irreducible. Delete last `()` first -> `()) (`? Actually `())(()` positions: 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' 5 '(' 6 ')'. Delete last "()" positions 5-6 -> `()) (`? String becomes `()) (`? chars 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' = `())(`? Wait original after deleting positions 5-6: positions 1-4 = '(' ')' ')' '(' = `())(`. Then delete first "()" -> `)(`. Irreducible. So fail.
What about `())(()` if delete positions 2-3? positions 2-3 = ')(' no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should fail on a mismatched close even if the stack top is an opening of different type but there is an opening of the same type below. We argued impossible. Let's test a constructed string that might seem possible: `[ ( ] )` no. `[ ( ] [ ] )` no. `[ ( ] ( ) )` no. `[ ( ] < > )` no. `[ ( ] { } )` no. It seems any mismatched close creates an irreducible "block".

Let's try to use a reduction sequence to save a mismatched close:
String = `[ ( ] )` no adjacent pair.
String = `[ ( ] ( ) )` has `()` at positions 4-5? Let's write `[ ( ] ( ) )` = '[' '(' ']' '(' ')' ')'. Adjacent "()" at positions 4-5. Delete -> `[ ( ] )` = `[(])` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] < > )` = '[' '(' ']' '<' '>' ')'. Delete "<>" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] [ ] )` = '[' '(' ']' '[' ']' ')'. Delete "[]" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] ( [ ] ) )`? Delete inner "[]" -> `[ ( ] ( ) )` -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
It seems the mismatched close `]` after `(` remains after all inner deletions and blocks.

What if the mismatched close is of the same type as an opening below, and the top opening is deleted by a later close that appears before the mismatched close? Wait later close appears after mismatched close, so to delete top opening, the mismatched close must be deleted first. Circular. So impossible.

Thus stack correct.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "possibly zero" operations: If S is empty, stack empty -> Yes. But constraints length >=1. If S is a single opening, final stack not empty -> No. Good.

Let's think about if there is a need to use `ord` or something? No.

Now, let's maybe consider a potential counterexample to stack algorithm from the rewriting system's confluence: The stack algorithm fails on `())(` because after processing `()` stack empty, then `)` fails. But the word `())(` has a redex at positions 1-2. If we reduce it, we get `)(`, irreducible. If we reduce a different redex? There is no other. So not reducible. Good.
What about `())(()`? Stack fails at second `)`. Reducible? Delete first `()` -> `)(()`; then delete last `()` -> `)(`; irreducible. Delete last `()` first -> `()) (`? Actually `())(()` positions: 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' 5 '(' 6 ')'. Delete last "()" positions 5-6 -> `()) (`? String becomes `()) (`? chars 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' = `())(`? Wait original after deleting positions 5-6: positions 1-4 = '(' ')' ')' '(' = `())(`. Then delete first "()" -> `)(`. Irreducible. So fail.
What about `())(()` if delete positions 2-3? positions 2-3 = ')(' no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should fail on a mismatched close even if the stack top is an opening of different type but there is an opening of the same type below. We argued impossible. Let's test a constructed string that might seem possible: `[ ( ] )` no. `[ ( ] [ ] )` no. `[ ( ] ( ) )` no. `[ ( ] < > )` no. `[ ( ] { } )` no. It seems any mismatched close creates an irreducible "block".

Let's try to use a reduction sequence to save a mismatched close:
String = `[ ( ] )` no adjacent pair.
String = `[ ( ] ( ) )` has `()` at positions 4-5? Let's write `[ ( ] ( ) )` = '[' '(' ']' '(' ')' ')'. Adjacent "()" at positions 4-5. Delete -> `[ ( ] )` = `[(])` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] < > )` = '[' '(' ']' '<' '>' ')'. Delete "<>" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] [ ] )` = '[' '(' ']' '[' ']' ')'. Delete "[]" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] ( [ ] ) )`? Delete inner "[]" -> `[ ( ] ( ) )` -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
It seems the mismatched close `]` after `(` remains after all inner deletions and blocks.

What if the mismatched close is of the same type as an opening below, and the top opening is deleted by a later close that appears before the mismatched close? Wait later close appears after mismatched close, so to delete top opening, the mismatched close must be deleted first. Circular. So impossible.

Thus stack correct.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "contiguous substring of T that is one of (), [], or <>" and "choose one such substring and delete it" - if there are overlapping possible substrings? They cannot overlap because a closing char is not an opening char. So no ambiguity.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `sys.stdin.readline().strip()` if the input has trailing spaces? It doesn't. If S could be " " no. Good.

Let's think about if `ch in "([<"` is O(1) for each char? String membership of length 3, fine. Could use `if ch == '(' or ch == '[' or ch == '<':` for speed. Both fine.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

If S="()" should be Yes even though only one color. The definition says colorful bracket sequence if possible to turn empty; no requirement to use all colors. So stack.

Let's consider if "colorful" might mean each adjacent pair deletion changes the color of neighboring brackets? No.

Now, let's maybe think about a more complex example: S = "([)]" no. S = "[([]])" no. S = "([<])>"? Let's test stack: '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'? Actually "([<])>" = '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'. Stack: '(' push, '[' push, '<' push, ']' top '<' mismatch -> fail. No adjacent pair? "<]" no, "])" no. Fail. Good.
S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.
S = "([<>) ]"? no.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Let's think if there is a known AtCoder problem "AGC 052 B - Tree Edges XOR" no. "AGC 052 C - Nondivisible Set" no. "AGC 052 D - Equal LIS" no. "AGC 052 E - 3 Letters" no. "AGC 052 F - Tree Degree Optimization" no. Not relevant.

Now, let's consider if the operation can delete "()" even if the parentheses are not "matched" in the usual sense because there is no stack? Example "())("? Delete "()" at positions 1-2 -> ")(" no. Not empty. Stack fail. Good.

Let's maybe test a string that stack algorithm says Yes but maybe no adjacent pair initially? We said impossible. Let's prove: If stack algorithm succeeds and string nonempty, consider the first character. It must be an opening. The matching close for that first character is found at some position. The substring between them is stack-valid. If it's empty, then the first two chars are "()" adjacent. If not, recursively the first char of the inner substring has an adjacent pair. So there is an adjacent pair. Thus reduction possible.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should be modified to handle "colorful" by using a stack of pairs and when a closing bracket appears, if it matches top, pop; else if top is an opening of different type, maybe we can "skip" the closing? No, sample 2 confirms no.

Let's consider if there is a possibility of deleting a pair that is not at the top of stack but after deleting some inner pairs, the top changes. Stack algorithm handles by popping inner pairs when their closes appear. If a close appears and matches top, pop. If it doesn't match top, fail. This is exactly the standard algorithm.

Now, let's think about if the input string could be very long and recursion not used. Fine.

Let's prepare final code. But the user asked "Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way." They might expect the final answer to include code only, but maybe they want the reasoning? The instruction says "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be code block only. We'll comply.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `sys.stdin.readline().strip()` if the input has trailing spaces? It doesn't. If S could be " " no. Good.

Let's think about if `ch in "([<"` is O(1) for each char? String membership of length 3, fine. Could use `if ch == '(' or ch == '[' or ch == '<':` for speed. Both fine.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's double-check sample 1 with code: Yes.

Now, let's think if there is any hidden "colorful" requirement that the sequence must use all three colors? No, sample 1 uses all three, sample 3 "())" no. If S="()" should be Yes even though only one color. The definition says colorful bracket sequence if possible to turn empty; no requirement to use all colors. So stack.

Let's consider if "colorful" might mean each adjacent pair deletion changes the color of neighboring brackets? No.

Now, let's maybe think about a more complex example: S = "([)]" no. S = "[([]])" no. S = "([<])>"? Let's test stack: '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'? Actually "([<])>" = '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'. Stack: '(' push, '[' push, '<' push, ']' top '<' mismatch -> fail. No adjacent pair? "<]" no, "])" no. Fail. Good.
S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.
S = "([<>) ]"? no.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm can be implemented with a list and dictionary. Good.

Let's consider if there is any need to handle `S` possibly containing `\r\n`; `strip()` removes `\r`. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "possibly zero" operations: If S is empty, stack empty -> Yes. But constraints length >=1. If S is a single opening, final stack not empty -> No. Good.

Let's think about if there is a need to use `ord` or something? No.

Now, let's maybe consider a potential counterexample to stack algorithm from the rewriting system's confluence: The stack algorithm fails on `())(` because after processing `()` stack empty, then `)` fails. But the word `())(` has a redex at positions 1-2. If we reduce it, we get `)(`, irreducible. If we reduce a different redex? There is no other. So not reducible. Good.
What about `())(()`? Stack fails at second `)`. Reducible? Delete first `()` -> `)(()`; then delete last `()` -> `)(`; irreducible. Delete last `()` first -> `()) (`? Actually `())(()` positions: 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' 5 '(' 6 ')'. Delete last "()" positions 5-6 -> `()) (`? String becomes `()) (`? chars 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' = `())(`? Wait original after deleting positions 5-6: positions 1-4 = '(' ')' ')' '(' = `())(`. Then delete first "()" -> `)(`. Irreducible. So fail.
What about `())(()` if delete positions 2-3? positions 2-3 = ')(' no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should fail on a mismatched close even if the stack top is an opening of different type but there is an opening of the same type below. We argued impossible. Let's test a constructed string that might seem possible: `[ ( ] )` no. `[ ( ] [ ] )` no. `[ ( ] ( ) )` no. `[ ( ] < > )` no. `[ ( ] { } )` no. It seems any mismatched close creates an irreducible "block".

Let's try to use a reduction sequence to save a mismatched close:
String = `[ ( ] )` no adjacent pair.
String = `[ ( ] ( ) )` has `()` at positions 4-5? Let's write `[ ( ] ( ) )` = '[' '(' ']' '(' ')' ')'. Adjacent "()" at positions 4-5. Delete -> `[ ( ] )` = `[(])` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] < > )` = '[' '(' ']' '<' '>' ')'. Delete "<>" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] [ ] )` = '[' '(' ']' '[' ']' ')'. Delete "[]" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] ( [ ] ) )`? Delete inner "[]" -> `[ ( ] ( ) )` -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
It seems the mismatched close `]` after `(` remains after all inner deletions and blocks.

What if the mismatched close is of the same type as an opening below, and the top opening is deleted by a later close that appears before the mismatched close? Wait later close appears after mismatched close, so to delete top opening, the mismatched close must be deleted first. Circular. So impossible.

Thus stack correct.

Let's think if there is a known AtCoder problem "AGC 052 B - Tree Edges XOR" no. "AGC 052 C - Nondivisible Set" no. "AGC 052 D - Equal LIS" no. "AGC 052 E - 3 Letters" no. "AGC 052 F - Tree Degree Optimization" no. Not relevant.

Now, let's consider if the operation can delete "()" even if the parentheses are not "matched" in the usual sense because there is no stack? Example "())("? Delete "()" at positions 1-2 -> ")(" no. Not empty. Stack fail. Good.

Let's maybe test a string that stack algorithm says Yes but maybe no adjacent pair initially? We said impossible. Let's prove: If stack algorithm succeeds and string nonempty, consider the first character. It must be an opening. The matching close for that first character is found at some position. The substring between them is stack-valid. If it's empty, then the first two chars are "()" adjacent. If not, recursively the first char of the inner substring has an adjacent pair. So there is an adjacent pair. Thus reduction possible.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should be modified to handle "colorful" by using a stack of pairs and when a closing bracket appears, if it matches top, pop; else if top is an opening of different type, maybe we can "skip" the closing? No, sample 2 confirms no.

Let's consider if there is a possibility of deleting a pair that is not at the top of stack but after deleting some inner pairs, the top changes. Stack algorithm handles by popping inner pairs when their closes appear. If a close appears and matches top, pop. If it doesn't match top, fail. This is exactly the standard algorithm.

Now, let's think about if the input string could be very long and recursion not used. Fine.

Let's prepare final code. But the user asked "Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way." They might expect the final answer to include code only, but maybe they want the reasoning? The instruction says "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be code block only. We'll comply.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `sys.stdin.readline().strip()` if the input has trailing spaces? It doesn't. If S could be " " no. Good.

Let's think about if `ch in "([<"` is O(1) for each char? String membership of length 3, fine. Could use `if ch == '(' or ch == '[' or ch == '<':` for speed. Both fine.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's double-check sample 1 with code: Yes.

Now, let's think if there is any hidden "colorful" requirement that the sequence must use all three colors? No, sample 1 uses all three, sample 3 "())" no. If S="()" should be Yes even though only one color. The definition says colorful bracket sequence if possible to turn empty; no requirement to use all colors. So stack.

Let's consider if "colorful" might mean each adjacent pair deletion changes the color of neighboring brackets? No.

Now, let's maybe think about a more complex example: S = "([)]" no. S = "[([]])" no. S = "([<])>"? Let's test stack: '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'? Actually "([<])>" = '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'. Stack: '(' push, '[' push, '<' push, ']' top '<' mismatch -> fail. No adjacent pair? "<]" no, "])" no. Fail. Good.
S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.
S = "([<>) ]"? no.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm can be implemented with a list and dictionary. Good.

Let's consider if there is any need to handle `S` possibly containing `\r\n`; `strip()` removes `\r`. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "possibly zero" operations: If S is empty, stack empty -> Yes. But constraints length >=1. If S is a single opening, final stack not empty -> No. Good.

Let's think about if there is a need to use `ord` or something? No.

Now, let's maybe consider a potential counterexample to stack algorithm from the rewriting system's confluence: The stack algorithm fails on `())(` because after processing `()` stack empty, then `)` fails. But the word `())(` has a redex at positions 1-2. If we reduce it, we get `)(`, irreducible. If we reduce a different redex? There is no other. So not reducible. Good.
What about `())(()`? Stack fails at second `)`. Reducible? Delete first `()` -> `)(()`; then delete last `()` -> `)(`; irreducible. Delete last `()` first -> `()) (`? Actually `())(()` positions: 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' 5 '(' 6 ')'. Delete last "()" positions 5-6 -> `()) (`? String becomes `()) (`? chars 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' = `())(`? Wait original after deleting positions 5-6: positions 1-4 = '(' ')' ')' '(' = `())(`. Then delete first "()" -> `)(`. Irreducible. So fail.
What about `())(()` if delete positions 2-3? positions 2-3 = ')(' no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should fail on a mismatched close even if the stack top is an opening of different type but there is an opening of the same type below. We argued impossible. Let's test a constructed string that might seem possible: `[ ( ] )` no. `[ ( ] [ ] )` no. `[ ( ] ( ) )` no. `[ ( ] < > )` no. `[ ( ] { } )` no. It seems any mismatched close creates an irreducible "block".

Let's try to use a reduction sequence to save a mismatched close:
String = `[ ( ] )` no adjacent pair.
String = `[ ( ] ( ) )` has `()` at positions 4-5? Let's write `[ ( ] ( ) )` = '[' '(' ']' '(' ')' ')'. Adjacent "()" at positions 4-5. Delete -> `[ ( ] )` = `[(])` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] < > )` = '[' '(' ']' '<' '>' ')'. Delete "<>" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] [ ] )` = '[' '(' ']' '[' ']' ')'. Delete "[]" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] ( [ ] ) )`? Delete inner "[]" -> `[ ( ] ( ) )` -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
It seems the mismatched close `]` after `(` remains after all inner deletions and blocks.

What if the mismatched close is of the same type as an opening below, and the top opening is deleted by a later close that appears before the mismatched close? Wait later close appears after mismatched close, so to delete top opening, the mismatched close must be deleted first. Circular. So impossible.

Thus stack correct.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "contiguous substring of T that is one of (), [], or <>" and "choose one such substring and delete it" - if there are overlapping possible substrings? They cannot overlap because a closing char is not an opening char. So no ambiguity.

Let's think if there is a known AtCoder problem "AGC 052 B - Tree Edges XOR" no. "AGC 052 C - Nondivisible Set" no. "AGC 052 D - Equal LIS" no. "AGC 052 E - 3 Letters" no. "AGC 052 F - Tree Degree Optimization" no. Not relevant.

Now, let's consider if the operation can delete "()" even if the parentheses are not "matched" in the usual sense because there is no stack? Example "())("? Delete "()" at positions 1-2 -> ")(" no. Not empty. Stack fail. Good.

Let's maybe test a string that stack algorithm says Yes but maybe no adjacent pair initially? We said impossible. Let's prove: If stack algorithm succeeds and string nonempty, consider the first character. It must be an opening. The matching close for that first character is found at some position. The substring between them is stack-valid. If it's empty, then the first two chars are "()" adjacent. If not, recursively the first char of the inner substring has an adjacent pair. So there is an adjacent pair. Thus reduction possible.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should be modified to handle "colorful" by using a stack of pairs and when a closing bracket appears, if it matches top, pop; else if top is an opening of different type, maybe we can "skip" the closing? No, sample 2 confirms no.

Let's consider if there is a possibility of deleting a pair that is not at the top of stack but after deleting some inner pairs, the top changes. Stack algorithm handles by popping inner pairs when their closes appear. If a close appears and matches top, pop. If it doesn't match top, fail. This is exactly the standard algorithm.

Now, let's think about if the input string could be very long and recursion not used. Fine.

Let's prepare final code. But the user asked "Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way." They might expect the final answer to include code only, but maybe they want the reasoning? The instruction says "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be code block only. We'll comply.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `sys.stdin.readline().strip()` if the input has trailing spaces? It doesn't. If S could be " " no. Good.

Let's think about if `ch in "([<"` is O(1) for each char? String membership of length 3, fine. Could use `if ch == '(' or ch == '[' or ch == '<':` for speed. Both fine.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Let's double-check sample 1 with code: Yes.

Now, let's think if there is any hidden "colorful" requirement that the sequence must use all three colors? No, sample 1 uses all three, sample 3 "())" no. If S="()" should be Yes even though only one color. The definition says colorful bracket sequence if possible to turn empty; no requirement to use all colors. So stack.

Let's consider if "colorful" might mean each adjacent pair deletion changes the color of neighboring brackets? No.

Now, let's maybe think about a more complex example: S = "([)]" no. S = "[([]])" no. S = "([<])>"? Let's test stack: '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'? Actually "([<])>" = '(' '[' '<' ']' ')' '>'. Stack: '(' push, '[' push, '<' push, ']' top '<' mismatch -> fail. No adjacent pair? "<]" no, "])" no. Fail. Good.
S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.
S = "([<>) ]"? no.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm can be implemented with a list and dictionary. Good.

Let's consider if there is any need to handle `S` possibly containing `\r\n`; `strip()` removes `\r`. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "possibly zero" operations: If S is empty, stack empty -> Yes. But constraints length >=1. If S is a single opening, final stack not empty -> No. Good.

Let's think about if there is a need to use `ord` or something? No.

Now, let's maybe consider a potential counterexample to stack algorithm from the rewriting system's confluence: The stack algorithm fails on `())(` because after processing `()` stack empty, then `)` fails. But the word `())(` has a redex at positions 1-2. If we reduce it, we get `)(`, irreducible. If we reduce a different redex? There is no other. So not reducible. Good.
What about `())(()`? Stack fails at second `)`. Reducible? Delete first `()` -> `)(()`; then delete last `()` -> `)(`; irreducible. Delete last `()` first -> `()) (`? Actually `())(()` positions: 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' 5 '(' 6 ')'. Delete last "()" positions 5-6 -> `()) (`? String becomes `()) (`? chars 1 '(' 2 ')' 3 ')' 4 '(' = `())(`? Wait original after deleting positions 5-6: positions 1-4 = '(' ')' ')' '(' = `())(`. Then delete first "()" -> `)(`. Irreducible. So fail.
What about `())(()` if delete positions 2-3? positions 2-3 = ')(' no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm should fail on a mismatched close even if the stack top is an opening of different type but there is an opening of the same type below. We argued impossible. Let's test a constructed string that might seem possible: `[ ( ] )` no. `[ ( ] [ ] )` no. `[ ( ] ( ) )` no. `[ ( ] < > )` no. `[ ( ] { } )` no. It seems any mismatched close creates an irreducible "block".

Let's try to use a reduction sequence to save a mismatched close:
String = `[ ( ] )` no adjacent pair.
String = `[ ( ] ( ) )` has `()` at positions 4-5? Let's write `[ ( ] ( ) )` = '[' '(' ']' '(' ')' ')'. Adjacent "()" at positions 4-5. Delete -> `[ ( ] )` = `[(])` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] < > )` = '[' '(' ']' '<' '>' ')'. Delete "<>" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] [ ] )` = '[' '(' ']' '[' ']' ')'. Delete "[]" -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
String = `[ ( ] ( [ ] ) )`? Delete inner "[]" -> `[ ( ] ( ) )` -> `[ ( ] )` no. Fail.
It seems the mismatched close `]` after `(` remains after all inner deletions and blocks.

What if the mismatched close is of the same type as an opening below, and the top opening is deleted by a later close that appears before the mismatched close? Wait later close appears after mismatched close, so to delete top opening, the mismatched close must be deleted first. Circular. So impossible.

Thus stack correct.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "contiguous substring of T that is one of (), [], or <>" and "choose one such substring and delete it" - if there are overlapping possible substrings? They cannot overlap because a closing char is not an opening char. So no ambiguity.

Before final, let's maybe consider if there is any issue with using `sys.stdin.readline().strip()` if the input has trailing spaces? It doesn't. If S could be " " no. Good.

Let's think about if `ch in "([<"` is O(1) for each char? String membership of length 3, fine. Could use `if ch == '(' or ch == '[' or ch == '<':` for speed. Both fine.

Let's write final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}
    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()
    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

S = "([<>])" = '(' '[' '<' '>' ']' ')'. Stack: '(' '[' '<' '>' pop '<', ']' pop '[', ')' pop '(' success. Reducible: delete "<>" -> "([])" -> "[]" -> "()" -> empty. Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of reducible => grammar one more time to ensure no hidden assumption about the first character. Suppose first char is '(' and it is deleted with a ')' that is not the first matching close in the reduction? The first char is at the beginning; when it is deleted, the pair is at the beginning of the current string. The ')' is immediately after it. The substring between them in the original string must have been deleted. But what if some characters before the first char? None. What if the ')' that matches the first char in the reduction is not the same as the ')' that is immediately after it at deletion time? It must be, because the pair deleted is "()", so the two characters are adjacent. The ')' is the character immediately to the right of '(' at that moment. In the original string, that ')' is some character after the first char. All characters between them have been deleted. Thus A reducible. The suffix after that ')' may have been partially deleted before, but the remaining operations after deleting the pair will reduce the suffix to empty. However, could some characters in the suffix have been deleted before the first pair deletion, and the remaining suffix is not exactly the original suffix? The original suffix B after the matching ')' might have had some deletions before; but the operations after the first pair deletion will reduce whatever remains of B to empty. Does that imply the original B is reducible? If B had some deletions before, those deletions plus the later operations form a reduction of B to empty. Yes, because deletions inside B before the first pair deletion are independent of the prefix? Could a deletion before the first pair deletion involve one char in B and one char outside B (between first '(' and matching ')')? No, because the first '(' and matching ')' are not deleted yet and separate regions. A deletion cannot cross them. So B reducible. Good.

Thus grammar proof solid.

Now, let's think about if the stack algorithm's success is equivalent to grammar. The grammar is:
L -> ε | L L | '(' L ')' | '[' L ']' | '<' L '>'
Actually the decomposition S = '(' A ')' B with A,B in L is equivalent to L -> '(' L ')' L, which with L -> ε generates the same as L -> LL | '(' L ')'? Let's see: L -> '(' L ')' L is a right-linear? It generates all balanced sequences. It is equivalent to L -> LL | '(' L ')' because any string generated by L -> '(' L ')' L can be parsed as '(' L ')' concatenated with L, and '(' L ')' is generated by L -> '(' L ')' if the inner L is generated. Conversely, LL with one factor '(' L ')' can be written as '(' L ')' L. So yes.
Stack algorithm is the standard parser for this grammar.

import sys

def main():
    S = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    stack = []
    matching = {')': '(', ']': '[', '>': '<'}

    for ch in S:
        if ch in "([<":
            stack.append(ch)
        else:
            if not stack or stack[-1] != matching[ch]:
                print("No")
                return
            stack.pop()

    print("Yes" if not stack else "No")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
